<p><b>Equatorial Guinea</b> (<a href="/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language">Spanish</a>: <i lang="es">Guinea Ecuatorial</i>;<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/French_language" title="French language">French</a>: <i lang="fr">Guinée équatoriale</i>; <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_language" title="Portuguese language">Portuguese</a>: <i lang="pt">Guiné Equatorial</i>), also rarely known as <b>Equatoguinea</b>, officially the <b>Republic of Equatorial Guinea</b> (Spanish: <i lang="es">República de Guinea Ecuatorial</i>, French: <i lang="fr">République de Guinée équatoriale</i>, Portuguese: <i lang="pt">República da Guiné Equatorial</i>),<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> is a country on the west coast of <a href="/wiki/Central_Africa" title="Central Africa">Central Africa</a>, with an area of 28,000 square kilometres (11,000&#160;sq&#160;mi). Formerly the colony of <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Guinea" title="Spanish Guinea">Spanish Guinea</a>, its post-independence name refers to its location near both the <a href="/wiki/Equator" title="Equator">Equator</a> and in the <a href="/wiki/Guinea_(region)" title="Guinea (region)">African region of Guinea</a>. As of 2021<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Equatorial_Guinea&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, the country had a population of 1,468,777,<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> over 85% of whom are members of the <a href="/wiki/Fang_people" title="Fang people">Fang people</a>, the country's dominant ethnic group. The <a href="/wiki/Bubi_people" title="Bubi people">Bubi people</a>, indigenous to Bioko are the second largest group at approximately 6.5% of the population.
</p><p>Equatorial Guinea consists of two parts, an insular and a mainland region. The <a href="/wiki/Islands_of_Equatorial_Guinea" class="mw-redirect" title="Islands of Equatorial Guinea">insular region</a> consists of the islands of <a href="/wiki/Bioko" title="Bioko">Bioko</a> (formerly <i>Fernando Pó</i>) in the Gulf of Guinea and <a href="/wiki/Annob%C3%B3n_Province" class="mw-redirect" title="Annobón Province">Annobón</a>, a small volcanic island which is the only part of the country south of the <a href="/wiki/Equator" title="Equator">equator</a>. Bioko Island is the northernmost part of Equatorial Guinea and is the site of the country's capital, <a href="/wiki/Malabo" title="Malabo">Malabo</a>. The Portuguese-speaking island nation of <a href="/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Tom%C3%A9_and_Pr%C3%ADncipe" title="São Tomé and Príncipe">São Tomé and Príncipe</a> is located between Bioko and Annobón. 
</p><p>The mainland region, <a href="/wiki/R%C3%ADo_Muni" title="Río Muni">Río Muni</a>, is bordered by <a href="/wiki/Cameroon" title="Cameroon">Cameroon</a> on the north and <a href="/wiki/Gabon" title="Gabon">Gabon</a> on the south and east. It is the location of <a href="/wiki/Bata,_Equatorial_Guinea" title="Bata, Equatorial Guinea">Bata</a>, Equatorial Guinea's second largest city, and <a href="/wiki/Ciudad_de_la_Paz" title="Ciudad de la Paz">Ciudad de la Paz</a>, the country's planned future capital. Rio Muni also includes several small offshore islands, such as <a href="/wiki/Corisco" title="Corisco">Corisco</a>, <a href="/wiki/Elobey_Grande" title="Elobey Grande">Elobey Grande</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Elobey_Chico" title="Elobey Chico">Elobey Chico</a>. The country is a member of the <a href="/wiki/African_Union" title="African Union">African Union</a>, <a href="/wiki/Organisation_internationale_de_la_Francophonie" title="Organisation internationale de la Francophonie">Francophonie</a>, <a href="/wiki/OPEC" title="OPEC">OPEC</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Community_of_Portuguese_Language_Countries" title="Community of Portuguese Language Countries">CPLP</a>.
</p><p>After becoming independent from Spain in 1968, Equatorial Guinea was ruled by <a href="/wiki/President_for_life" title="President for life">President for life</a> <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Mac%C3%ADas_Nguema" title="Francisco Macías Nguema">Francisco Macías Nguema</a> until he was overthrown in a <a href="/wiki/1979_Equatorial_Guinea_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="1979 Equatorial Guinea coup d&#39;état">coup in 1979</a> by his nephew <a href="/wiki/Teodoro_Obiang_Nguema_Mbasogo" title="Teodoro Obiang Nguema Mbasogo">Teodoro Obiang Nguema Mbasogo</a> who has served as the country's president since. Both presidents have been widely characterized as dictators by foreign observers. Since the mid-1990s, Equatorial Guinea has become one of <a href="/wiki/Sub-Saharan_Africa" title="Sub-Saharan Africa">sub-Saharan Africa</a>'s largest oil producers.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> It has subsequently become the richest country per capita in Africa,<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> and its <a href="/wiki/Gross_domestic_product" title="Gross domestic product">gross domestic product</a> (GDP) adjusted for <a href="/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity" title="Purchasing power parity">purchasing power parity</a> (PPP) per capita ranks 43rd in the world;<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> however, the wealth is distributed extremely unevenly, with few people benefiting from the oil riches. The country ranks 144th on the 2019 <a href="/wiki/Human_Development_Index" title="Human Development Index">Human Development Index</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-HDI2_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HDI2-17">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> with less than half the population having access to clean drinking water and 7.9% of children dying before the age of five.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Equatorial_Guinea_profile_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Equatorial_Guinea_profile-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As a former Spanish colony, the country maintains Spanish as its official language alongside French and (as of 2010) Portuguese,<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> being the only African country (aside from the largely unrecognized <a href="/wiki/Sahrawi_Arab_Democratic_Republic" title="Sahrawi Arab Democratic Republic">Sahrawi Arab Democratic Republic</a>) where Spanish is an official language.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> It is also the most widely spoken language (considerably more than the other two official languages); according to the <a href="/wiki/Instituto_Cervantes" title="Instituto Cervantes">Instituto Cervantes</a>, 87.7% of the population has a good command of Spanish.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Equatorial Guinea's government is authoritarian and has one of the <a href="/wiki/Human_rights_in_Equatorial_Guinea" title="Human rights in Equatorial Guinea">worst human rights records in the world</a>, consistently ranking among the "worst of the worst" in <a href="/wiki/Freedom_House" title="Freedom House">Freedom House</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Freedom_in_the_World" title="Freedom in the World">annual survey of political and civil rights</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Reporters_Without_Borders" title="Reporters Without Borders">Reporters Without Borders</a> ranks Obiang among its "predators" of press freedom.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Human_trafficking_in_Equatorial_Guinea" title="Human trafficking in Equatorial Guinea">Human trafficking</a> is a significant problem, with the U.S. Trafficking in Persons Report identifying Equatorial Guinea as a source and destination country for <a href="/wiki/Unfree_labor" class="mw-redirect" title="Unfree labor">forced labour</a>  and sex trafficking. The report also noted that Equatorial Guinea "does not fully meet the minimum standards for the elimination of trafficking but is making significant efforts to do so."<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Equatorial_Guinea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: History">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Equatorial_Guinea" title="History of Equatorial Guinea">History of Equatorial Guinea</a></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Pygmy_peoples" title="Pygmy peoples">Pygmies</a> probably once lived in the continental region that is now Equatorial Guinea, but are today found only in isolated pockets in southern Río Muni. <a href="/wiki/Bantu_languages" title="Bantu languages">Bantu</a> migrations started probably around 2,000 BC from between south-east Nigeria and north-west Cameroon (the Grassfields).<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> They must have settled continental Equatorial Guinea around 500 BC at the latest.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> The earliest settlements on Bioko Island are dated to AD 530.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Annobonese_Creole" title="Annobonese Creole">Annobón</a> population, originally native to <a href="/wiki/Angola" title="Angola">Angola</a>, was introduced by the Portuguese via <a href="/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Tom%C3%A9_island" class="mw-redirect" title="São Tomé island">São Tomé island</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="First_European_contact_and_Portuguese_rule_.281472.E2.80.931778.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="First_European_contact_and_Portuguese_rule_(1472–1778)">First European contact and Portuguese rule (1472–1778)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Equatorial_Guinea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: First European contact and Portuguese rule (1472–1778)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:1729_West_Africa_map_(Cameroon_%26_Nigeria).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/1729_West_Africa_map_%28Cameroon_%26_Nigeria%29.jpg/220px-1729_West_Africa_map_%28Cameroon_%26_Nigeria%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="168" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/1729_West_Africa_map_%28Cameroon_%26_Nigeria%29.jpg/330px-1729_West_Africa_map_%28Cameroon_%26_Nigeria%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/1729_West_Africa_map_%28Cameroon_%26_Nigeria%29.jpg/440px-1729_West_Africa_map_%28Cameroon_%26_Nigeria%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="466" data-file-height="355" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Empire" title="Portuguese Empire">Portuguese</a> rule in Equatorial Guinea lasted from the arrival of <a href="/wiki/Fern%C3%A3o_do_P%C3%B3" title="Fernão do Pó">Fernão do Pó</a> (Fernando Pó) in 1472 until the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_El_Pardo_(1778)" title="Treaty of El Pardo (1778)">1778 Treaty of El Pardo</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Empire" title="Portuguese Empire">Portuguese explorer</a> <a href="/wiki/Fern%C3%A3o_do_P%C3%B3" title="Fernão do Pó">Fernando Pó</a>, seeking a path to India, is credited as being the first European to see the island of Bioko, in 1472. He called it <i>Formosa</i> ("Beautiful"), but it quickly took on the name of its European discoverer. <a href="/wiki/Fernando_P%C3%B3_(island)" class="mw-redirect" title="Fernando Pó (island)">Fernando Pó</a> and Annobón were colonized by Portugal in 1474. The first factories were established on the islands around 1500 as the Portuguese quickly recognized the positives of the islands including volcanic soil and disease-resistant highlands. Despite natural advantages, initial Portuguese efforts in 1507 to establish a sugarcane plantation and town near what is now Concepción on Fernando Pó failed due to Bubi hostility and fever.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> The main island's rainy climate, extreme humidity and temperature swings took a major toll on European settlers from the beginning, and it would be centuries before attempts restarted.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Early_Spanish_rule_and_lease_to_Britain_.281778.E2.80.931844.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_Spanish_rule_and_lease_to_Britain_(1778–1844)">Early Spanish rule and lease to Britain (1778–1844)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Equatorial_Guinea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Early Spanish rule and lease to Britain (1778–1844)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1778, Queen <a href="/wiki/Maria_I_of_Portugal" title="Maria I of Portugal">Maria I of Portugal</a> and King <a href="/wiki/Charles_III_of_Spain" title="Charles III of Spain">Charles III of Spain</a> signed the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_El_Pardo_(1778)" title="Treaty of El Pardo (1778)">Treaty of El Pardo</a> which ceded <a href="/wiki/Bioko" title="Bioko">Bioko</a>, adjacent islets, and commercial rights to the <a href="/wiki/Bight_of_Bonny" class="mw-redirect" title="Bight of Bonny">Bight of Biafra</a> between the <a href="/wiki/Niger_River" title="Niger River">Niger</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ogoue_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Ogoue River">Ogoue</a> rivers to <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Empire" title="Spanish Empire">Spain</a> in exchange for large areas in South America that are now Western Brazil. Brigadier Felipe José, Count of Arjelejos formally took possession of Bioko from Portugal on 21 October 1778. After sailing for Annobón to take possession, the Count died of disease caught on Bioko and the fever-ridden crew mutinied. The crew landed on São Tomé instead where they were imprisoned by the Portuguese authorities after having lost over 80% of their men to sickness.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> As a result of this disaster, Spain was thereafter hesitant to invest heavily in its new possession. However, despite the setback Spaniards began to use the island as a base for slave trading on the nearby mainland. Between 1778 and 1810, the territory of what became Equatorial Guinea was administered by the <a href="/wiki/Viceroyalty_of_the_R%C3%ADo_de_la_Plata" title="Viceroyalty of the Río de la Plata">Viceroyalty of the Río de la Plata</a>, based in <a href="/wiki/Buenos_Aires" title="Buenos Aires">Buenos Aires</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Fegley,_Randall_1989_p._6-7_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fegley,_Randall_1989_p._6-7-32">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Unwilling to invest heavily in the development of Fernando Pó, from 1827 to 1843, the Spanish leased a base at Malabo on <a href="/wiki/Bioko" title="Bioko">Bioko</a> to the <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland" title="United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland">United Kingdom</a> which it had sought as part of its efforts to suppress the <a href="/wiki/Atlantic_slave_trade" title="Atlantic slave trade">transatlantic slave trade</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> Without Spanish permission, the British moved the headquarters of the Mixed Commission for the Suppression of Slave Traffic to Fernando Pó in 1827, before moving it back to <a href="/wiki/Sierra_Leone" title="Sierra Leone">Sierra Leone</a> under an agreement with Spain in 1843. Spain's decision to abolish slavery in 1817 at British insistence damaged the colony's perceived value to the authorities and so leasing naval bases was an effective revenue earner from an otherwise unprofitable possession.<sup id="cite_ref-Fegley,_Randall_1989_p._6-7_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fegley,_Randall_1989_p._6-7-32">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> An agreement by Spain to sell its African colony to the British was cancelled in 1841 due to metropolitan public opinion and opposition by Spanish Congress.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Late_19th_century_.281844.E2.80.931900.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Late_19th_century_(1844–1900)">Late 19th century (1844–1900)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Equatorial_Guinea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Late 19th century (1844–1900)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:(1897)_Golfo_de_Guinea.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/%281897%29_Golfo_de_Guinea.jpg/220px-%281897%29_Golfo_de_Guinea.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="314" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/%281897%29_Golfo_de_Guinea.jpg/330px-%281897%29_Golfo_de_Guinea.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/%281897%29_Golfo_de_Guinea.jpg/440px-%281897%29_Golfo_de_Guinea.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2473" data-file-height="3535" /></a><figcaption>Map of the Spanish possessions in 1897, before the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1900)" title="Treaty of Paris (1900)">Treaty of Paris (1900)</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1844, the British returned the island to Spanish control and the area became known as the "Territorios Españoles del Golfo de Guinea". Due to epidemics, Spain did not invest much in the colony, and in 1862 an outbreak of yellow fever killed many of the whites that had settled on the island. Despite this, plantations continued to be established by private citizens through the second half of the 19th century.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated3_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated3-35">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Plantation" title="Plantation">plantations</a> of <a href="/wiki/Bioko" title="Bioko">Fernando Pó</a> were mostly run by a black <a href="/wiki/Creole_elite" class="mw-redirect" title="Creole elite">Creole elite</a>, later known as <a href="/wiki/Fernandino_peoples" title="Fernandino peoples">Fernandinos</a>. The British settled some 2,000 Sierra Leoneans and freed slaves there during their rule, and a trickle of immigration from West Africa and the West Indies continued after the British left. A number of freed Angolan slaves, Portuguese-African creoles and immigrants from Nigeria and Liberia also began to be settled in the colony where they quickly began to join the new group.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-36">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> To the local mix were added Cubans, Filipinos, Jews and Spaniards of various colours, many of whom had been deported to Africa for political or other crimes, as well as some settlers backed by the government.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By 1870 the prognosis of whites that lived on the island was much improved after recommendations that they live in the highlands, and by 1884 much of the minimal administrative machinery and key plantations had moved to <a href="/wiki/Pico_Basil%C3%A9" title="Pico Basilé">Basile</a> hundreds of meters above sea level. <a href="/wiki/Henry_Morton_Stanley" title="Henry Morton Stanley">Henry Morton Stanley</a> had labeled Fernando Pó "a jewel which Spain did not polish" for refusing to enact such a policy. Despite the improved survival chances of Europeans living on the island, <a href="/wiki/Mary_Kingsley" title="Mary Kingsley">Mary Kingsley</a>, who was staying on the island still described Fernando Pó as "a more uncomfortable form of execution" for Spaniards appointed there.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated3_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated3-35">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There was also a trickle of immigration from the neighboring Portuguese islands, escaped slaves, and prospective planters. Although a few of the <a href="/wiki/Fernandino_peoples" title="Fernandino peoples">Fernandinos</a> were Catholic and Spanish-speaking, about nine-tenths of them were Protestant and English-speaking on the eve of the First World War, and <a href="/wiki/Pidgin_English" class="mw-redirect" title="Pidgin English">pidgin English</a> was the <i><a href="/wiki/Lingua_franca" title="Lingua franca">lingua franca</a></i> of the island. The Sierra Leoneans were particularly well placed as planters while labor recruitment on the <a href="/wiki/Ivory_Coast" title="Ivory Coast">Windward coast</a> continued, for they kept family and other connections there and could easily arrange a supply of labor. The Fernandinos proved to become effective traders and middlemen between the natives and Europeans.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_36-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-36">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> A freed slave from the West Indies by way of <a href="/wiki/Sierra_Leone" title="Sierra Leone">Sierra Leone</a> named William Pratt established the cocoa crop on Fernando Pó.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Early_20th_century_.281900.E2.80.931945.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_20th_century_(1900–1945)">Early 20th century (1900–1945)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Equatorial_Guinea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Early 20th century (1900–1945)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Guinea" title="Spanish Guinea">Spanish Guinea</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Eq_Guinea_1900_ES.PNG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fc/Eq_Guinea_1900_ES.PNG/220px-Eq_Guinea_1900_ES.PNG" decoding="async" width="220" height="237" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/f/fc/Eq_Guinea_1900_ES.PNG 1.5x" data-file-width="330" data-file-height="355" /></a><figcaption>Borders after the agreement of 1900 on the land that would become <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Guinea" title="Spanish Guinea">Spanish Guinea</a>, until the independence of 1968</figcaption></figure>
<p>Spain had not occupied the large area in the <a href="/wiki/Bight_of_Biafra" title="Bight of Biafra">Bight of Biafra</a> to which it had right by <a href="/wiki/Treaty" title="Treaty">treaty</a>, and the French had busily expanded their occupation at the expense of the territory claimed by Spain. Madrid only partly backed the explorations of men like <a href="/wiki/Manuel_Iradier" title="Manuel Iradier">Manuel Iradier</a> who had signed treaties in the interior as far as Gabon and Cameroon, leaving much of the land out of "effective occupation" as demanded by the terms of the 1885 <a href="/wiki/Berlin_Conference" title="Berlin Conference">Berlin Conference</a>. More important events such as the conflict in Cuba and the eventual <a href="/wiki/Spanish%E2%80%93American_War" title="Spanish–American War">Spanish–American War</a> kept Madrid busy at an inopportune moment. Minimal government backing for mainland annexation came as a result of public opinion and a need for labour on Fernando Pó.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The eventual <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1900)" title="Treaty of Paris (1900)">treaty of Paris</a> in 1900 left Spain with the continental <a href="/wiki/Enclave_and_exclave" title="Enclave and exclave">enclave</a> of Rio Muni, a mere 26,000&#160;km<sup>2</sup> out of the 300,000 stretching east to the <a href="/wiki/Ubangi_River" title="Ubangi River">Ubangi river</a> which the Spaniards had initially claimed.<sup id="cite_ref-Clarence-Smith_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clarence-Smith-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> The tiny enclave was far smaller than what the Spaniards had considered themselves rightfully entitled to under their claims and the Treaty of El Pardo. The humiliation of the Franco-Spanish negotiations, combined with the disaster in Cuba led to the head of the Spanish negotiating team, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Pedro_Gover_y_Tovar&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Pedro Gover y Tovar (page does not exist)">Pedro Gover y Tovar</a> committing suicide on the voyage home on 21 October 1901.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2-41">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> Iradier himself died in despair in 1911, and it would be decades before his achievements would be recognised by Spanish popular opinion when the port of <a href="/wiki/Cogo,_Equatorial_Guinea" title="Cogo, Equatorial Guinea">Cogo</a> was renamed Puerto Iradier in his honour.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The opening years of the twentieth century saw a new generation of Spanish immigrants. Land regulations issued in 1904–1905 favoured Spaniards, and most of the later big planters arrived from Spain after that.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (November 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> An agreement made with Liberia in 1914 to import cheap labor greatly favoured wealthy men with ready access to the state, and the shift in labor supplies from Liberia to Río Muni increased this advantage. Due to malpractice however, the Liberian government eventually ended the treaty after embarrassing revelations about the state of Liberian workers on Fernando Pó in the Christy Report which brought down the country's president <a href="/wiki/Charles_D._B._King" title="Charles D. B. King">Charles D. B. King</a> in 1930.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (November 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Corisco-Saliendo_de_misa-1910.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Corisco-Saliendo_de_misa-1910.jpg/220px-Corisco-Saliendo_de_misa-1910.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="137" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Corisco-Saliendo_de_misa-1910.jpg/330px-Corisco-Saliendo_de_misa-1910.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Corisco-Saliendo_de_misa-1910.jpg/440px-Corisco-Saliendo_de_misa-1910.jpg 2x" data-file-width="489" data-file-height="304" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Corisco" title="Corisco">Corisco</a> in 1910</figcaption></figure>
<p>The greatest constraint to <a href="/wiki/Economic_development" title="Economic development">economic development</a> was a chronic shortage of labour. Pushed into the interior of the island and decimated by alcohol addiction, venereal disease, <a href="/wiki/Smallpox" title="Smallpox">smallpox</a>, and <a href="/wiki/African_trypanosomiasis" title="African trypanosomiasis">sleeping sickness</a>, the indigenous <a href="/wiki/Bubi_people" title="Bubi people">Bubi</a> population of <a href="/wiki/Bioko" title="Bioko">Bioko</a> refused to work on <a href="/wiki/Plantation" title="Plantation">plantations</a>. Working their own small <a href="/wiki/Cocoa_bean" title="Cocoa bean">cocoa</a> farms gave them a considerable degree of autonomy.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>By the late nineteenth century, the Bubi were protected from the demands of the planters by Spanish <a href="/wiki/Claretians" title="Claretians">Claretian</a> missionaries, who were very influential in the colony and eventually organised the Bubi into little mission theocracies reminiscent of the famous <a href="/wiki/Jesuit" class="mw-redirect" title="Jesuit">Jesuit</a> <a href="/wiki/Reducciones" class="mw-redirect" title="Reducciones">reductions</a> in <a href="/wiki/Paraguay" title="Paraguay">Paraguay</a>. Catholic penetration was furthered by two small insurrections in 1898 and 1910 protesting <a href="/wiki/Conscription" title="Conscription">conscription</a> of <a href="/wiki/Forced_labor" class="mw-redirect" title="Forced labor">forced labour</a> for the plantations. The Bubi were disarmed in 1917, and left dependent on the missionaries.<sup id="cite_ref-Clarence-Smith_40-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clarence-Smith-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Serious labour shortages were temporarily solved by a massive influx of refugees from German <a href="/wiki/Kamerun" title="Kamerun">Kamerun</a>, along with thousands of white German soldiers who stayed on the island for several years.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2_41-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2-41">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Between 1926 and 1959 Bioko and Rio Muni were united as the colony of <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Guinea" title="Spanish Guinea">Spanish Guinea</a>. The economy was based on large <a href="/wiki/Cocoa_bean" title="Cocoa bean">cacao</a> and <a href="/wiki/Coffee" title="Coffee">coffee</a> plantations and <a href="/wiki/Logging" title="Logging">logging</a> concessions and the workforce was mostly immigrant <a href="/wiki/Contract_labour" class="mw-redirect" title="Contract labour">contract labour</a> from <a href="/wiki/Liberia" title="Liberia">Liberia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Nigeria" title="Nigeria">Nigeria</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Cameroun" class="mw-redirect" title="Cameroun">Cameroun</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Between 1914 and 1930, an estimated 10,000 Liberians went to Fernando Po under a labour treaty that was stopped altogether in 1930.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With Liberian workers no longer available, planters of Fernando Po turned to Rio Muni. Campaigns were mounted to subdue the <a href="/wiki/Fang_people" title="Fang people">Fang people</a> in the 1920s, at the time that Liberia was beginning to cut back on recruitment. There were garrisons of the colonial guard throughout the enclave by 1926, and the whole colony was considered 'pacified' by 1929.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Iberia-_vuelo_inaugural_a_Bata_(Guinea)_(1941)_(5811105541).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Iberia-_vuelo_inaugural_a_Bata_%28Guinea%29_%281941%29_%285811105541%29.jpg/220px-Iberia-_vuelo_inaugural_a_Bata_%28Guinea%29_%281941%29_%285811105541%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="140" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Iberia-_vuelo_inaugural_a_Bata_%28Guinea%29_%281941%29_%285811105541%29.jpg/330px-Iberia-_vuelo_inaugural_a_Bata_%28Guinea%29_%281941%29_%285811105541%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Iberia-_vuelo_inaugural_a_Bata_%28Guinea%29_%281941%29_%285811105541%29.jpg/440px-Iberia-_vuelo_inaugural_a_Bata_%28Guinea%29_%281941%29_%285811105541%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2746" data-file-height="1748" /></a><figcaption>Inaugural flight with <a href="/wiki/Iberia_(airline)" title="Iberia (airline)">Iberia</a> from <a href="/wiki/Madrid" title="Madrid">Madrid</a> to <a href="/wiki/Bata,_Equatorial_Guinea" title="Bata, Equatorial Guinea">Bata</a>, 1941</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Civil_War" title="Spanish Civil War">Spanish Civil War</a> had a major impact on the colony. A group of 150 Spanish whites, including the Governor-General and Vice-Governor-General of Río Muni, created a socialist party called the Popular Front in the enclave which served to oppose the interests of the Fernando Pó plantation owners. When the War broke out <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Franco" title="Francisco Franco">Francisco Franco</a> ordered Nationalist forces based in the Canaries to ensure control over Equatorial Guinea. In September 1936 Nationalist forces backed by Falangists from Fernando Pó, similarly to what happened in Spain proper, took control of Río Muni, which under Governor-General Luiz Sanchez Guerra Saez and his deputy Porcel had backed the Republican government. By November the Popular Front and its supporters had been defeated and Equatorial Guinea secured for Franco. The commander in charge of the occupation, Juan Fontán Lobé, was appointed Governor-General by Franco and began to exert more effective Spanish control over the enclave interior.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Rio Muni had a small population, officially a little over 100,000 in the 1930s, and escape across the frontiers into <a href="/wiki/Cameroun" class="mw-redirect" title="Cameroun">Cameroun</a> or <a href="/wiki/Gabon" title="Gabon">Gabon</a> was very easy. Also, the <a href="/wiki/Timber" class="mw-redirect" title="Timber">timber</a> companies needed increasing numbers of workers, and the spread of <a href="/wiki/Coffee" title="Coffee">coffee</a> cultivation offered an alternative means of paying taxes.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="margin-left:0.1em; white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify" title="Wikipedia:Please clarify"><span title="The text near this tag may need clarification or removal of jargon. (November 2016)">clarification needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Fernando Pó thus continued to suffer from labour shortages. The French only briefly permitted recruitment in Cameroun, and the main source of labour came to be <a href="/wiki/Igbo_people" title="Igbo people">Igbo</a> smuggled in canoes from <a href="/wiki/Calabar" title="Calabar">Calabar</a> in <a href="/wiki/Nigeria" title="Nigeria">Nigeria</a>. This resolution to the worker shortage allowed Fernando Pó to become one of Africa's most productive agricultural areas after the <a href="/wiki/Second_World_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Second World War">Second World War</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Clarence-Smith_40-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clarence-Smith-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Final_years_of_Spanish_rule_.281945.E2.80.931968.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Final_years_of_Spanish_rule_(1945–1968)">Final years of Spanish rule (1945–1968)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Equatorial_Guinea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Final years of Spanish rule (1945–1968)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Centro_Cultural_de_Espa%C3%B1a_en_Malabo.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/Centro_Cultural_de_Espa%C3%B1a_en_Malabo.jpg/220px-Centro_Cultural_de_Espa%C3%B1a_en_Malabo.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/Centro_Cultural_de_Espa%C3%B1a_en_Malabo.jpg/330px-Centro_Cultural_de_Espa%C3%B1a_en_Malabo.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/Centro_Cultural_de_Espa%C3%B1a_en_Malabo.jpg/440px-Centro_Cultural_de_Espa%C3%B1a_en_Malabo.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1327" data-file-height="885" /></a><figcaption>Centro Cultural de España (Cultural Centre of Spain) in Malabo</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Fraga_na_sinatura_da_independencia_de_Guinea_Ecuatorial.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Fraga_na_sinatura_da_independencia_de_Guinea_Ecuatorial.jpg/220px-Fraga_na_sinatura_da_independencia_de_Guinea_Ecuatorial.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="162" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Fraga_na_sinatura_da_independencia_de_Guinea_Ecuatorial.jpg/330px-Fraga_na_sinatura_da_independencia_de_Guinea_Ecuatorial.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Fraga_na_sinatura_da_independencia_de_Guinea_Ecuatorial.jpg/440px-Fraga_na_sinatura_da_independencia_de_Guinea_Ecuatorial.jpg 2x" data-file-width="620" data-file-height="456" /></a><figcaption>Signing of the independence of Equatorial Guinea by the Spanish minister <a href="/wiki/Manuel_Fraga" title="Manuel Fraga">Manuel Fraga</a> together with the new Equatorial Guinean president <a href="/wiki/Mac%C3%ADas_Nguema" class="mw-redirect" title="Macías Nguema">Macías Nguema</a> on 12 October 1968</figcaption></figure>
<p>Politically, post-war colonial history has three fairly distinct phases: up to 1959, when its status was raised from "colonial" to "provincial", following the approach of the <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Empire" title="Portuguese Empire">Portuguese Empire</a>; between 1960 and 1968, when Madrid attempted a partial <a href="/wiki/Decolonisation" class="mw-redirect" title="Decolonisation">decolonisation</a> aimed at keeping the territory as part of the Spanish system; and from 1968 on, after the territory became an independent <a href="/wiki/Republic" title="Republic">republic</a>. The first phase consisted of little more than a continuation of previous policies; these closely resembled the policies of Portugal and France, notably in dividing the population into a vast majority governed as 'natives' or non-citizens, and a very small minority (together with whites) admitted to civic status as <i><a href="/wiki/Emancipados" title="Emancipados">emancipados</a></i>, <a href="/wiki/Cultural_assimilation" title="Cultural assimilation">assimilation</a> to the metropolitan culture being the only permissible means of advancement.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>This "provincial" phase saw the beginnings of <a href="/wiki/Nationalism" title="Nationalism">nationalism</a>, but chiefly among small groups who had taken refuge from the <i><a href="/wiki/Caudillo" title="Caudillo">Caudillo</a></i><span class="nowrap" style="padding-left:0.1em;">&#39;</span>s paternal hand in Cameroun and Gabon. They formed two bodies: the <a href="/wiki/Atanasio_Ndongo_Miyone" title="Atanasio Ndongo Miyone">Movimiento Nacional de Liberación de la Guinea</a> (MONALIGE), and the <a href="/wiki/Idea_Popular_de_Guinea_Ecuatorial" class="mw-redirect" title="Idea Popular de Guinea Ecuatorial">Idea Popular de Guinea Ecuatorial</a> (IPGE). The pressure they could bring to bear was weak, but the general trend in West Africa was not, and by the late 1960s much of the African continent had been granted independence. Aware of this trend, the Spanish began to increase efforts to prepare the country for independence and massively stepped up development. The Gross National Product per capita in 1965 was $466, which was the highest in black Africa, and the Spanish constructed an international airport at Santa Isabel, a television station and increased the literacy rate to a relatively high 89%. At the same time measures were taken to battle sleeping sickness and leprosy in the enclave, and by 1967 the number of hospital beds per capita in Equatorial Guinea was higher than Spain itself, with 1637 beds in 16 hospitals. All the same, measures to improve education floundered, and like in the <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_Congo" class="mw-redirect" title="Democratic Republic of Congo">Democratic Republic of Congo</a> by the end of colonial rule the number of Africans in higher education was in only the double digits, and political education necessary to a functioning state was negligible.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A decision of 9 August 1963, approved by a referendum of 15 December 1963, gave the territory a measure of autonomy and the administrative promotion of a 'moderate' group, the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Movimiento_de_Uni%C3%B3n_Nacional_de_Guinea_Ecuatorial&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Movimiento de Unión Nacional de Guinea Ecuatorial (page does not exist)">Movimiento de Unión Nacional de Guinea Ecuatorial</a><span class="noprint" style="font-size:85%; font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Movimiento_de_Uni%C3%B3n_Nacional_de_Guinea_Ecuatorial" class="extiw" title="es:Movimiento de Unión Nacional de Guinea Ecuatorial">es</a>&#93;</span> (MUNGE). This proved a feeble instrument, and, with growing pressure for change from the UN, Madrid was gradually forced to give way to the currents of nationalism. Two General Assembly resolutions were passed in 1965 ordering Spain to grant independence to the colony, and in 1966 a UN Commission toured the country before recommending the same thing. In response, the Spanish declared that they would hold a constitutional convention on 27 October 1967 to negotiate a new constitution for an independent Equatorial Guinea. The conference was attended by 41 local delegates and 25 Spaniards. The Africans were principally divided between Fernandinos and Bubi on one side, who feared a loss of privileges and 'swamping' by the Fang majority, and the Río Muni Fang nationalists on the other. At the conference the leading Fang figure, the later first president <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Mac%C3%ADas_Nguema" title="Francisco Macías Nguema">Francisco Macías Nguema</a> gave a controversial speech in which he claimed that <a href="/wiki/Adolf_Hitler" title="Adolf Hitler">Adolf Hitler</a> had "saved Africa".<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> After nine sessions the conference was suspended due to deadlock between the "unionists" and "separatists" who wanted a separate Fernando Pó. Macías resolved to travel to the UN to bolster international awareness of the issue, and his firebrand speeches in New York contributed to Spain naming a date for both independence and general elections. In July 1968 virtually all Bubi leaders went to the UN in New York to try and raise awareness for their cause, but the world community was uninterested in quibbling over the specifics of colonial independence. The 1960s were a time of great optimism over the future of the former African colonies, and groups that had been close to European rulers, like the Bubi, were not viewed positively.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Independence_under_Mac.C3.ADas_.281968.E2.80.931979.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Independence_under_Macías_(1968–1979)">Independence under Macías (1968–1979)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Equatorial_Guinea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Independence under Macías (1968–1979)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Don_Francisco_Macias.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Don_Francisco_Macias.jpg/170px-Don_Francisco_Macias.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="250" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Don_Francisco_Macias.jpg/255px-Don_Francisco_Macias.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Don_Francisco_Macias.jpg/340px-Don_Francisco_Macias.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1455" data-file-height="2139" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Francisco_Mac%C3%ADas_Nguema" title="Francisco Macías Nguema">Francisco Macías Nguema</a>, first <a href="/wiki/President_of_Equatorial_Guinea" class="mw-redirect" title="President of Equatorial Guinea">president of Equatorial Guinea</a> in 1968, became a dictator until he was overthrown in a coup d'état in 1979.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Independence from Spain was gained on 12 October 1968, at noon in the capital, Malabo. The new country became the Republic of Equatorial Guinea (the date is celebrated as the country's <a href="/wiki/List_of_national_independence_days" title="List of national independence days">Independence Day</a><sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>). Macías became president in the country's <a href="/wiki/1968_Spanish_Guinean_general_election" title="1968 Spanish Guinean general election">only free and fair election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> The Spanish (ruled by <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Franco" title="Francisco Franco">Franco</a>) had backed Macías in the election due to his perceived loyalty, however while on the campaign trail he had proven to be far less easy to handle than they had expected. Much of his campaigning involved visiting rural areas of Río Muni and promising young Fang that they would have the houses and wives of the Spanish if they voted for him. In the towns he had instead presented himself as the urbane leader who had bested the Spanish at the UN, and he had won in the second round of voting; greatly helped by the vote-splitting of his rivals.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The euphoria of independence became quickly overshadowed by problems emanating from the <a href="/wiki/Nigerian_Civil_War" title="Nigerian Civil War">Nigerian Civil War</a>. Fernando Pó was inhabited by many Biafra-supporting Ibo migrant workers and many refugees from the breakaway state fled to the island, straining it to breaking point. The <a href="/wiki/International_Committee_of_the_Red_Cross" title="International Committee of the Red Cross">International Committee of the Red Cross</a> began running relief flights out of Equatorial Guinea, but Macías quickly became spooked and shut the flights down, refusing to allow them to fly diesel fuel for their trucks nor oxygen tanks for medical operations. Very quickly the Biafran separatists were starved into submission without international backing.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the Public Prosecutor complained about "excesses and maltreatment" by government officials, Macías had 150 alleged coup-plotters executed in a purge on Christmas Eve 1969, all of whom happened to be political opponents.<sup id="cite_ref-tufts_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tufts-53">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Macias Nguema further consolidated his <a href="/wiki/Totalitarianism" title="Totalitarianism">totalitarian powers</a> by <a href="/wiki/One-party_state" title="One-party state">outlawing opposition political parties</a> in July 1970 and making himself <a href="/wiki/President_for_life" title="President for life">president for life</a> in 1972.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> He broke off ties with Spain and the West. In spite of his condemnation of <a href="/wiki/Marxism" title="Marxism">Marxism</a>, which he deemed "<a href="/wiki/Neo-colonialism" class="mw-redirect" title="Neo-colonialism">neo-colonialist</a>", Equatorial Guinea maintained very special relations with <a href="/wiki/Communist_state" title="Communist state">communist states</a>, notably China, Cuba, <a href="/wiki/East_Germany" title="East Germany">East Germany</a> and the <a href="/wiki/USSR" class="mw-redirect" title="USSR">USSR</a>. Macias Nguema signed a preferential <a href="/wiki/Trade_agreement" title="Trade agreement">trade agreement</a> and a shipping treaty with the Soviet Union. The Soviets also made loans to Equatorial Guinea.<sup id="cite_ref-aworawo103_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-aworawo103-56">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The shipping agreement gave the Soviets permission for a pilot <a href="/wiki/Fishery" title="Fishery">fishery</a> development project and also a naval base at <a href="/wiki/Luba,_Equatorial_Guinea" title="Luba, Equatorial Guinea">Luba</a>. In return the USSR was to supply fish to Equatorial Guinea. China and Cuba also gave different forms of financial, military, and technical assistance to Equatorial Guinea, which got them a measure of influence there. For the USSR, there was an advantage to be gained in the <a href="/wiki/Angola_Civil_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Angola Civil War">War in Angola</a> from access to Luba base and later on to <a href="/wiki/Malabo_International_Airport" title="Malabo International Airport">Malabo International Airport</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-aworawo103_56-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-aworawo103-56">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1974, the <a href="/wiki/World_Council_of_Churches" title="World Council of Churches">World Council of Churches</a> affirmed that large numbers of people had been murdered since 1968 in an ongoing <a href="/wiki/Revolutionary_terror" title="Revolutionary terror">reign of terror</a>. A quarter of the entire population had fled abroad, they said, while 'the prisons are overflowing and to all intents and purposes form one vast concentration camp'. Out of a population of 300,000, an estimated 80,000 were killed.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Apart from allegedly committing <a href="/wiki/Genocide" title="Genocide">genocide</a> against the ethnic minority <a href="/wiki/Bubi_people" title="Bubi people">Bubi people</a>, Macias Nguema ordered the deaths of thousands of suspected opponents, closed down churches and presided over the economy's collapse as skilled citizens and foreigners fled the country.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Obiang_.281979.E2.80.93present.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Obiang_(1979–present)">Obiang (1979–present)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Equatorial_Guinea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Obiang (1979–present)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Teodoro_Obiang_Nguema_Mbasogo_with_Obamas_2014.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Teodoro_Obiang_Nguema_Mbasogo_with_Obamas_2014.jpg/220px-Teodoro_Obiang_Nguema_Mbasogo_with_Obamas_2014.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Teodoro_Obiang_Nguema_Mbasogo_with_Obamas_2014.jpg/330px-Teodoro_Obiang_Nguema_Mbasogo_with_Obamas_2014.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Teodoro_Obiang_Nguema_Mbasogo_with_Obamas_2014.jpg/440px-Teodoro_Obiang_Nguema_Mbasogo_with_Obamas_2014.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3392" data-file-height="2544" /></a><figcaption>Obiang and U.S. president <a href="/wiki/Barack_Obama" title="Barack Obama">Obama</a> with their wives in 2014</figcaption></figure>
<p>The nephew of Macías Nguema, <a href="/wiki/Teodoro_Obiang_Nguema_Mbasogo" title="Teodoro Obiang Nguema Mbasogo">Teodoro Obiang</a> deposed his uncle on 3 August 1979, in a bloody <i><a href="/wiki/1979_Equatorial_Guinea_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="1979 Equatorial Guinea coup d&#39;état">coup d'état</a></i>; over two weeks of civil war ensued until Macías Nguema was captured. He was tried and executed soon afterward, with Obiang succeeding him as a less bloody, but still authoritarian president.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1995 <a href="/wiki/Mobil" title="Mobil">Mobil</a>, an American oil company, discovered oil in Equatorial Guinea. The country subsequently experienced rapid economic development, but earnings from the country's oil wealth have not reached the population and the country ranks low on the UN human development index. 7.9% of children die before the age of 5 and more than 50% of the population lacks access to clean <a href="/wiki/Drinking_water" title="Drinking water">drinking water</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Equatorial_Guinea_profile_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Equatorial_Guinea_profile-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Teodoro_Obiang_Nguema_Mbasogo" title="Teodoro Obiang Nguema Mbasogo">President Teodoro Obiang</a> is widely suspected of using the country's oil wealth to enrich himself<sup id="cite_ref-global_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-global-60">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> and his associates. In 2006, Forbes estimated his personal wealth at $600 million.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2011, the government announced it was planning a new capital for the country, named <a href="/wiki/Oyala" class="mw-redirect" title="Oyala">Oyala</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> The city was renamed <a href="/wiki/Ciudad_de_la_Paz" title="Ciudad de la Paz">Ciudad de la Paz</a> (<i>"City of Peace"</i>) in 2017.
</p><p>As of February&#160;2016<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Equatorial_Guinea&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, Obiang is Africa's second-longest serving dictator after <a href="/wiki/Cameroon" title="Cameroon">Cameroon</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Paul_Biya" title="Paul Biya">Paul Biya</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Equatorial Guinea was elected as a non-permanent member of the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council" title="United Nations Security Council">United Nations Security Council</a> 2018–19.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>On 7 March 2021, there were <a href="/wiki/2021_Bata_explosions" title="2021 Bata explosions">munition explosions</a> at a military base near the city of <a href="/wiki/Bata,_Equatorial_Guinea" title="Bata, Equatorial Guinea">Bata</a> causing 98 deaths and 600 people being injured and treated at the hospital.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In November 2022 Obiang was re-elected in the <a href="/wiki/2022_Equatorial_Guinean_general_election" title="2022 Equatorial Guinean general election">2022 Equatorial Guinean general election</a> with 99.7% of the vote amid accusations of fraud by the opposition.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Government_and_politics">Government and politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Equatorial_Guinea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Government and politics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Politics_of_Equatorial_Guinea" title="Politics of Equatorial Guinea">Politics of Equatorial Guinea</a> and <a href="/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Equatorial_Guinea" title="Foreign relations of Equatorial Guinea">Foreign relations of Equatorial Guinea</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Autobahnbau_in_Oyala.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Autobahnbau_in_Oyala.JPG/220px-Autobahnbau_in_Oyala.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Autobahnbau_in_Oyala.JPG/330px-Autobahnbau_in_Oyala.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Autobahnbau_in_Oyala.JPG/440px-Autobahnbau_in_Oyala.JPG 2x" data-file-width="4000" data-file-height="3000" /></a><figcaption>Highway construction in <a href="/wiki/Ciudad_de_la_Paz" title="Ciudad de la Paz">Ciudad de la Paz</a> in 2010. Ciudad de la Paz will be the future <a href="/wiki/Capital_city" title="Capital city">capital</a> of Equatorial Guinea.</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Presidential_palace_in_malabo_equatorial_guinea.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/Presidential_palace_in_malabo_equatorial_guinea.png/220px-Presidential_palace_in_malabo_equatorial_guinea.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="124" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/Presidential_palace_in_malabo_equatorial_guinea.png/330px-Presidential_palace_in_malabo_equatorial_guinea.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/Presidential_palace_in_malabo_equatorial_guinea.png/440px-Presidential_palace_in_malabo_equatorial_guinea.png 2x" data-file-width="3185" data-file-height="1792" /></a><figcaption>Presidential palace of Teodoro Obiang in Malabo</figcaption></figure>
<p>The current president of Equatorial Guinea is Teodoro Obiang. The 1982 constitution of Equatorial Guinea gives him extensive powers, including naming and dismissing members of the cabinet, making laws by decree, dissolving the Chamber of Representatives, negotiating and ratifying treaties and serving as commander in chief of the armed forces. Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Pascual_Obama_Asue" title="Francisco Pascual Obama Asue">Francisco Pascual Obama Asue</a> was appointed by Obiang and operates under powers delegated by the President.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Equatorial_Guinea_Map.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/Equatorial_Guinea_Map.png/220px-Equatorial_Guinea_Map.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="273" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/Equatorial_Guinea_Map.png/330px-Equatorial_Guinea_Map.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/Equatorial_Guinea_Map.png/440px-Equatorial_Guinea_Map.png 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="1240" /></a><figcaption>Map of Equatorial Guinea made by the <a href="/wiki/CIA" class="mw-redirect" title="CIA">CIA</a> in 1992</figcaption></figure>
<p>During the four decades of his rule, Obiang has shown little tolerance for opposition. While the country is nominally a multiparty democracy, its elections have generally been considered a sham. According to <a href="/wiki/Human_Rights_Watch" title="Human Rights Watch">Human Rights Watch</a>, the dictatorship of President Obiang used an <a href="/wiki/Oil_boom" title="Oil boom">oil boom</a> to entrench and enrich itself further at the expense of the country's people.<sup id="cite_ref-bbc.co.uk_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bbc.co.uk-70">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> Since August 1979 some 12 real and perceived unsuccessful coup attempts have occurred.<sup id="cite_ref-HRW_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HRW-71">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to a March 2004 BBC profile,<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> politics within the country were dominated by tensions between Obiang's son, <a href="/wiki/Teodoro_Nguema_Obiang_Mangue" title="Teodoro Nguema Obiang Mangue">Teodoro Nguema Obiang Mangue</a>, and other close relatives with powerful positions in the security forces. The tension may be rooted in a power shift arising from the dramatic increase in oil production which has occurred since 1997.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In 2004 a plane load of suspected mercenaries was intercepted in <a href="/wiki/Zimbabwe" title="Zimbabwe">Zimbabwe</a> while allegedly on the way to overthrow Obiang. A November 2004 report<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> named <a href="/wiki/Mark_Thatcher" title="Mark Thatcher">Mark Thatcher</a> as a financial backer of the <a href="/wiki/2004_Equatorial_Guinea_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat_attempt" class="mw-redirect" title="2004 Equatorial Guinea coup d&#39;état attempt">2004 Equatorial Guinea coup d'état attempt</a> organized by <a href="/wiki/Simon_Mann" title="Simon Mann">Simon Mann</a>. Various accounts also named the United Kingdom's <a href="/wiki/Secret_Intelligence_Service" class="mw-redirect" title="Secret Intelligence Service">MI6</a>, the United States' <a href="/wiki/Central_Intelligence_Agency" title="Central Intelligence Agency">CIA</a>, and Spain as tacit supporters of the coup attempt.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Nevertheless, the <a href="/wiki/Amnesty_International" title="Amnesty International">Amnesty International</a> report released in June 2005<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
on the ensuing trial of those allegedly involved highlighted the prosecution's failure to produce conclusive evidence that a coup attempt had actually taken place. Simon Mann was released from prison on 3 November 2009 for humanitarian reasons.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Since 2005, <a href="/wiki/Military_Professional_Resources_Inc." title="Military Professional Resources Inc.">Military Professional Resources Inc.</a>, a US-based international <a href="/wiki/Private_military_company" title="Private military company">private military company</a>, has worked in Equatorial Guinea to train police forces in appropriate human rights practices. In 2006, <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_State" title="United States Secretary of State">US Secretary of State</a> <a href="/wiki/Condoleezza_Rice" title="Condoleezza Rice">Condoleezza Rice</a> hailed Obiang as a "good friend" despite repeated criticism of his human rights and civil liberties record. The <a href="/wiki/US_Agency_for_International_Development" class="mw-redirect" title="US Agency for International Development">US Agency for International Development</a> entered into a memorandum of understanding (MOU) with Obiang, in April 2006, to establish a social development Fund in the country, implementing projects in the areas of health, education, women's affairs and the environment.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2006, Obiang signed an anti-torture decree banning all forms of abuse and improper treatment in Equatorial Guinea, and commissioned the renovation and modernization of Black Beach prison in 2007 to ensure the humane treatment of prisoners.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> However, human rights abuses have continued. Human Rights Watch and Amnesty International among other non-governmental organizations have documented severe human rights abuses in prisons, including torture, beatings, unexplained deaths and illegal detention.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In their most recently publishing findings (2020), <a href="/wiki/Transparency_International" title="Transparency International">Transparency International</a> awarded Equatorial Guinea a total score of 16 on their <a href="/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index" title="Corruption Perceptions Index">Corruption Perceptions Index</a> (CPI). CPI ranks countries by their perceived level of public corruption where zero is very corrupt and 100 is extremely clean. Equatorial Guinea was the 174th lowest scoring nation out of a total of 180 countries.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> Freedom House, a pro-democracy and human rights NGO, described Obiang as one of the world's "most kleptocratic living autocrats", and complained about the US government welcoming his administration and buying oil from it.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Obiang was re-elected to serve an additional term in 2009 in <a href="/wiki/Equatorial_Guinean_presidential_election,_2009" class="mw-redirect" title="Equatorial Guinean presidential election, 2009">an election</a> the <a href="/wiki/African_Union" title="African Union">African Union</a> deemed "in line with electoral law".<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Obiang re-appointed Prime Minister Ignacio Milam Tang in 2010.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Teodoro_Obiang_detail,_1650FRP051.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/Teodoro_Obiang_detail%2C_1650FRP051.jpg/170px-Teodoro_Obiang_detail%2C_1650FRP051.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="224" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/Teodoro_Obiang_detail%2C_1650FRP051.jpg/255px-Teodoro_Obiang_detail%2C_1650FRP051.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/Teodoro_Obiang_detail%2C_1650FRP051.jpg/340px-Teodoro_Obiang_detail%2C_1650FRP051.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1026" data-file-height="1351" /></a><figcaption>According to the BBC, President <a href="/wiki/Teodoro_Obiang_Nguema_Mbasogo" title="Teodoro Obiang Nguema Mbasogo">Obiang Nguema</a> "has been described by rights organisations as one of Africa's most brutal dictators."<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>In November 2011, a new constitution was approved. The vote on the constitution was taken though neither the text or its content was revealed to the public before the vote. Under the new constitution the president was limited to a maximum of two seven-year terms and would be both the head of state and head of the government, therefore eliminating the prime minister. The new constitution also introduced the figure of a vice president and called for the creation of a 70-member senate with 55 senators elected by the people and the 15 remaining designated by the president. In the following cabinet reshuffle it was announced that there would be two vice-presidents in clear violation of the constitution that was just taking effect.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In October 2012, during an interview with <a href="/wiki/Christiane_Amanpour" title="Christiane Amanpour">Christiane Amanpour</a> on <a href="/wiki/CNN" title="CNN">CNN</a>, Obiang was asked whether he would step down at the end of the current term (2009–2016) since the new constitution limited the number of terms to two and he has been reelected at least 4 times. Obiang answered he refused to step aside because the new constitution was not retroactive and the two-term limit would only become applicable from 2016.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The elections on 26 May 2013 combined the senate, lower house and mayoral contests all in a single package. Like all previous elections, this was denounced by the opposition and it too was won by Obiang's PDGE. During the electoral contest, the ruling party hosted internal elections which were later scrapped as none of the president's favorite candidates led the internal lists. Ultimately, the ruling party and the satellites of the ruling coalition decided to run not based on the candidates but based on the party. This created a situation where during the election the ruling party's coalition did not provide the names of their candidates so effectively individuals were not running for office, instead, the party was the one running for office.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The May 2013 elections were marked by a series of events including the popular protest planned by a group of activists from the MPP (Movement of Popular Protest) which included several social and political groups. The MPP called for a peaceful protest at the Plaza de la Mujer square on 15 May. MPP coordinator Enrique Nsolo Nzo was arrested and official state media portrayed him as planning to destabilize the country and depose the president. However, despite speaking under duress and with clear signs of torture, Nsolo said that they had planned a peaceful protest and had indeed obtained all the legal authorizations required to carry out the peaceful protest. In addition to that, he firmly stated that he was not affiliated with any political party. The Plaza de la Mujer square in Malabo was occupied by the police from 13 May and it has been heavily guarded ever since. The government embarked on a <a href="/wiki/Censorship" title="Censorship">censorship</a> program that affected social sites including Facebook and other websites that were critical to the government of Equatorial Guinea. The censorship was implemented by redirecting online searches to the official government website.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Shortly after the elections, opposition party CPDS announced that they were going to protest peacefully against the 26 May elections on 25 June.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> Interior minister Clemente Engonga refused to authorise the protest on the grounds that it could "destabilize" the country and CPDS decided to go forward, claiming constitutional right. On the night of 24 June, the CPDS headquarters in Malabo were surrounded by heavily armed police officers to keep those inside from leaving and thus effectively blocking the protest. Several leading members of CPDS were detained in Malabo and others in Bata were kept from boarding several local flights to Malabo.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2016 Obiang <a href="/wiki/2016_Equatorial_Guinean_presidential_election" title="2016 Equatorial Guinean presidential election">was reelected</a> for an additional seven-year term, in an election which, according to <a href="/wiki/Freedom_House" title="Freedom House">Freedom House</a>, was plagued by police violence, detentions and torture against opposition factions.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following the <a href="/wiki/2022_Equatorial_Guinean_general_election" title="2022 Equatorial Guinean general election">2022 general elections</a>, President Obiang's <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Party_of_Equatorial_Guinea" title="Democratic Party of Equatorial Guinea">Democratic Party of Equatorial Guinea</a> holds all of the 100 seats in the Chamber of Deputies and all of those in the Senate. The opposition is almost non-existent in the country and is organized from Spain mainly within the social-democratic Convergence for Social Democracy. Most of the media are under state control; the private television channels, those of the Asonga group, belong to the president's family.<sup id="cite_ref-mondediplo.com_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-mondediplo.com-91">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Armed_forces">Armed forces</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Equatorial_Guinea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Armed forces">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Antonov_An-72P,_Equatorial_Guinea_-_Air_Force_AN1593110.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/Antonov_An-72P%2C_Equatorial_Guinea_-_Air_Force_AN1593110.jpg/220px-Antonov_An-72P%2C_Equatorial_Guinea_-_Air_Force_AN1593110.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/Antonov_An-72P%2C_Equatorial_Guinea_-_Air_Force_AN1593110.jpg/330px-Antonov_An-72P%2C_Equatorial_Guinea_-_Air_Force_AN1593110.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/Antonov_An-72P%2C_Equatorial_Guinea_-_Air_Force_AN1593110.jpg/440px-Antonov_An-72P%2C_Equatorial_Guinea_-_Air_Force_AN1593110.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1600" data-file-height="1067" /></a><figcaption>An <a href="/wiki/Antonov_An-72" title="Antonov An-72">Antonov An-72</a>P of the <a href="/wiki/Armed_Forces_of_Equatorial_Guinea" title="Armed Forces of Equatorial Guinea">Armed Forces of Equatorial Guinea</a> on lift off</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Armed_Forces_of_Equatorial_Guinea" title="Armed Forces of Equatorial Guinea">Armed Forces of Equatorial Guinea</a> consists of approximately 2,500 service members.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (February 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> The army has almost 1,400 soldiers, the police 400 paramilitary men, the navy 200 service members, and the air force about 120 members. There is also a <a href="/wiki/Gendarmerie" title="Gendarmerie">gendarmerie</a>, but the number of members is unknown. The Gendarmerie is a new branch of the service in which training and education is being supported by the French Military Cooperation in Equatorial Guinea.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Geography">Geography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Equatorial_Guinea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Geography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Equatorial_Guinea" title="Geography of Equatorial Guinea">Geography of Equatorial Guinea</a></div>
<p>Equatorial Guinea is on the west coast of <a href="/wiki/Central_Africa" title="Central Africa">Central Africa</a>. The country consists of a mainland territory, <a href="/wiki/R%C3%ADo_Muni" title="Río Muni">Río Muni</a>, which is bordered by Cameroon to the north and Gabon to the east and south, and five small islands, <a href="/wiki/Bioko" title="Bioko">Bioko</a>, <a href="/wiki/Corisco" title="Corisco">Corisco</a>, <a href="/wiki/Annob%C3%B3n" title="Annobón">Annobón</a>, <a href="/wiki/Elobey_Chico" title="Elobey Chico">Elobey Chico</a> (Small Elobey), and <a href="/wiki/Elobey_Grande" title="Elobey Grande">Elobey Grande</a> (Great Elobey).
